Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.

Like yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.

I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.

Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.

But I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.

And socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.

Sorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.

Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.

I mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Sorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.

I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.

I mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. And teaching, you’re constantly moving anyway. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything is kind of in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Though sometimes I’ll be standing at the board and realize I’ve been holding the marker cap for way too long—actually, that happens more when the students are all talking at once and I’m trying to remember who needed to go to the nurse and who lost their Chromebook login…\n\nSorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving much more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless, even when you're not particularly anxious or excited?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. Yeah… yes. It’s not just recent. It’s been kind of hanging around for, um… a little over two years now, I think. About two years and maybe two months.

It’s hard to tell because by the time I get home from school, my brain is just kind of… full. Like today one of my students spent ten minutes arguing that a pencil sharpener was “eating” his pencil, and then I had to call his mom about something completely different, so by evening I’m not exactly in a fun, relaxed mindset.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mostly just feel worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t walk around crying or anything, and at school I can still smile for the kids and do the lesson and make the little jokes you kind of have to make so they stay with you.\n\nBut underneath that, most days there’s this gray, flat feeling. It’s like it follows me home. I’ll look at my garden, or I’ll think about getting out my watercolors—things that used to feel close to me—and they just feel kind of far away, like I can see them but can’t quite get to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.\n\nLike yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Grading papers, making dinner, packing my bag, getting ready for the next morning… it all feels like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating more or less than usual or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in any clear way. My eating is kind of all over the place just because the school day is weird—sometimes lunch is like seven minutes standing by the copier, and sometimes I forget I packed yogurt and then find it warm later, which is… not great.\n\nBut weight-wise, I haven’t noticed anything big. My clothes seem to fit the same, I think. I haven’t been trying to change anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything is kind of in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Though sometimes I’ll be standing at the board and realize I’ve been holding the marker cap for way too long—actually, that happens more when the students are all talking at once and I’m trying to remember who needed to go to the nurse and who lost their Chromebook login…\n\nSorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving much more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless, even when you're not particularly anxious or excited?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. And teaching, you’re constantly moving anyway. Half the day I’m walking around the room or crouching next to someone’s desk, or trying to remember where I set down the attendance folder… which I swear disappears on its own.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel like I can’t sit still, and I don’t think people have said I’m moving unusually slowly either. It’s more that I feel worn out, if that makes sense, but not like my body is in slow motion or racing around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or really low, not just for a day or two, but for weeks at a time—maybe with some of the same feelings you're describing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just recent. It’s been kind of hanging around for, um… a little over two years now, I think. About two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt sort of started after my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. Not that it’s all his fault or anything—sorry, I don’t mean it like that. It was just… suddenly the rhythm of the house was different, and I was adjusting to work and home feeling different at the same time. And somewhere in there, I realized I’d been feeling low for weeks, then months, and then it just kind of became the background.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F33.1", "F33.2", "F33.3"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everyone does, I guess—end of the school year can make you feel like you’re running on fumes, and there was one year with a really difficult class where I cried in my car a couple times, which sounds dramatic, but teachers do that more than people think.

But I don’t know if I’d say it was depression for several weeks or anything clear like that. I’m having trouble separating normal stressful times from… whatever this is. So I guess I can’t really point to a definite episode before this one.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. I guess… I came in because I’m just tired all the time. I’m a teacher, so people always say, “Well, of course you’re tired,” and I get that—kids have endless energy, and then there’s grading, lesson plans, emails from parents, all of it. But this feels different.\n\nEven if I sleep through the night, I wake up feeling like I’m already behind, and by the time I get home I have to really push myself just to grade papers, make dinner, and get ready for the next morning. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, even activities you normally like, like hobbies, time with family, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I still do things after work sometimes, like I’ll put on a show or talk to my sister, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m not enjoying them. It’s hard to tell because by the time I get home from school, my brain is just kind of… full. Like today one of my students spent ten minutes arguing that a pencil sharpener was “eating” his pencil, and then I had to call his mom about something completely different, so by evening I’m not exactly in a fun, relaxed mindset.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mostly just feel worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t walk around crying or anything, and at school I can still smile for the kids and do the lesson and make the little jokes you kind of have to make so they stay with you.\n\nBut underneath that, most days there’s this gray, flat feeling. It’s like it follows me home. I’ll look at my garden, or I’ll think about getting out my watercolors—things that used to feel close to me—and they just feel kind of far away, like I can see them but can’t quite get to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.\n\nLike yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Grading papers, making dinner, packing my bag, getting ready for the next morning… it all feels like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating more or less than usual or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in any clear way. My eating is kind of all over the place just because the school day is weird—sometimes lunch is like seven minutes standing by the copier, and sometimes I forget I packed yogurt and then find it warm later, which is… not great.\n\nBut weight-wise, I haven’t noticed anything big. My clothes seem to fit the same, I think. I haven’t been trying to change anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything is kind of in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Though sometimes I’ll be standing at the board and realize I’ve been holding the marker cap for way too long—actually, that happens more when the students are all talking at once and I’m trying to remember who needed to go to the nurse and who lost their Chromebook login…\n\nSorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving much more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless, even when you're not particularly anxious or excited?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. And teaching, you’re constantly moving anyway. Half the day I’m walking around the room or crouching next to someone’s desk, or trying to remember where I set down the attendance folder… which I swear disappears on its own.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel like I can’t sit still, and I don’t think people have said I’m moving unusually slowly either. It’s more that I feel worn out, if that makes sense, but not like my body is in slow motion or racing around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or really low, not just for a day or two, but for weeks at a time—maybe with some of the same feelings you're describing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve had weight loss, no. At least not anything I’ve noticed or that my clothes would be telling me, you know? My jeans are still… unfortunately honest.

Appetite-wise, it’s kind of hard to say because school days are weird. Sometimes I don’t eat much lunch just because there are twelve things happening at once, and by the time I sit down someone needs help with a Chromebook or there’s a parent email I forgot to answer. But that’s been teaching forever, not really a new thing.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a significant decrease in appetite or lost weight without trying.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. I guess… I came in because I’m just tired all the time. But this feels different.\n\nEven if I sleep through the night, I wake up feeling like I’m already behind, and by the time I get home I have to really push myself just to grade papers, make dinner, and get ready for the next morning. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, even activities you normally like, like hobbies, time with family, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I still do things after work sometimes, like I’ll put on a show or talk to my sister, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m not enjoying them. It’s hard to tell because by the time I get home from school, my brain is just kind of… full. Like today one of my students spent ten minutes arguing that a pencil sharpener was “eating” his pencil, and then I had to call his mom about something completely different, so by evening I’m not exactly in a fun, relaxed mindset.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mostly just feel worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t walk around crying or anything, and at school I can still smile for the kids and do the lesson and make the little jokes you kind of have to make so they stay with you.\n\nBut underneath that, most days there’s this gray, flat feeling. It’s like it follows me home. I’ll look at my garden, or I’ll think about getting out my watercolors—things that used to feel close to me—and they just feel kind of far away, like I can see them but can’t quite get to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.\n\nLike yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Grading papers, making dinner, packing my bag, getting ready for the next morning… it all feels like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating more or less than usual or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in any clear way. My eating is kind of all over the place just because the school day is weird—sometimes lunch is like seven minutes standing by the copier, and sometimes I forget I packed yogurt and then find it warm later, which is… not great.\n\nBut weight-wise, I haven’t noticed anything big. My clothes seem to fit the same, I think. I haven’t been trying to change anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. But this feels different.\n\nEven if I sleep through the night, I wake up feeling like I’m already behind, and by the time I get home I have to really push myself just to grade papers, make dinner, and get ready for the next morning. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, even activities you normally like, like hobbies, time with family, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I still do things after work sometimes, like I’ll put on a show or talk to my sister, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m not enjoying them. It’s hard to tell because by the time I get home from school, my brain is just kind of… full. Like today one of my students spent ten minutes arguing that a pencil sharpener was “eating” his pencil, and then I had to call his mom about something completely different, so by evening I’m not exactly in a fun, relaxed mindset.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mostly just feel worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t walk around crying or anything, and at school I can still smile for the kids and do the lesson and make the little jokes you kind of have to make so they stay with you.\n\nBut underneath that, most days there’s this gray, flat feeling. It’s like it follows me home. I’ll look at my garden, or I’ll think about getting out my watercolors—things that used to feel close to me—and they just feel kind of far away, like I can see them but can’t quite get to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.\n\nLike yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Grading papers, making dinner, packing my bag, getting ready for the next morning… it all feels like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating more or less than usual or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in any clear way. My eating is kind of all over the place just because the school day is weird—sometimes lunch is like seven minutes standing by the copier, and sometimes I forget I packed yogurt and then find it warm later, which is… not great.\n\nBut weight-wise, I haven’t noticed anything big. My clothes seem to fit the same, I think. I haven’t been trying to change anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything is kind of in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Though sometimes I’ll be standing at the board and realize I’ve been holding the marker cap for way too long—actually, that happens more when the students are all talking at once and I’m trying to remember who needed to go to the nurse and who lost their Chromebook login…\n\nSorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving much more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless, even when you're not particularly anxious or excited?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. And teaching, you’re constantly moving anyway. Half the day I’m walking around the room or crouching next to someone’s desk, or trying to remember where I set down the attendance folder… which I swear disappears on its own.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel like I can’t sit still, and I don’t think people have said I’m moving unusually slowly either. It’s more that I feel worn out, if that makes sense, but not like my body is in slow motion or racing around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or really low, not just for a day or two, but for weeks at a time—maybe with some of the same feelings you're describing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just recent. It’s been kind of hanging around for, um… a little over two years now, I think. About two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt sort of started after my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. Not that it’s all his fault or anything—sorry, I don’t mean it like that. It was just… suddenly the rhythm of the house was different, and I was adjusting to work and home feeling different at the same time. And somewhere in there, I realized I’d been feeling low for weeks, then months, and then it just kind of became the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this current period—like feeling down, losing interest in things, or having low energy—that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everyone does, I guess—end of the school year can make you feel like you’re running on fumes, and there was one year with a really difficult class where I cried in my car a couple times, which sounds dramatic, but teachers do that more than people think.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it was depression for several weeks or anything clear like that. I’m having trouble separating normal stressful times from… whatever this is. So I guess I can’t really point to a definite episode before this one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss or a significant decrease in your appetite over the past two years, even if you weren't trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had weight loss, no. At least not anything I’ve noticed or that my clothes would be telling me, you know? My jeans are still… unfortunately honest.\n\nAppetite-wise, it’s kind of hard to say because school days are weird. Sometimes I don’t eat much lunch just because there are twelve things happening at once, and by the time I sit down someone needs help with a Chromebook or there’s a parent email I forgot to answer. But that’s been teaching forever, not really a new thing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a significant decrease in appetite or lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or think clearly during the past two years, even when you're not particularly stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, as a teacher I feel like my brain is always juggling seventeen things at once—attendance, lesson plans, someone needing a bandaid, someone crying because their pencil broke, and then an email comes in about a meeting that could’ve been an email… or was already an email, actually.\n\nSo I do have moments where I feel scattered, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s just the job. When I’m not stressed or tired… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear problem with focus, no. At least not something I could point to and say, “That’s new.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. I guess… I came in because I’m just tired all the time. I’m a teacher, so people always say, “Well, of course you’re tired,” and I get that—kids have endless energy, and then there’s grading, lesson plans, emails from parents, all of it. But this feels different.\n\nEven if I sleep through the night, I wake up feeling like I’m already behind, and by the time I get home I have to really push myself just to grade papers, make dinner, and get ready for the next morning. It’s like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, even activities you normally like, like hobbies, time with family, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I still do things after work sometimes, like I’ll put on a show or talk to my sister, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m not enjoying them. It’s hard to tell because by the time I get home from school, my brain is just kind of… full. Like today one of my students spent ten minutes arguing that a pencil sharpener was “eating” his pencil, and then I had to call his mom about something completely different, so by evening I’m not exactly in a fun, relaxed mindset.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I mostly just feel worn out, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when there’s no obvious reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t walk around crying or anything, and at school I can still smile for the kids and do the lesson and make the little jokes you kind of have to make so they stay with you.\n\nBut underneath that, most days there’s this gray, flat feeling. It’s like it follows me home. I’ll look at my garden, or I’ll think about getting out my watercolors—things that used to feel close to me—and they just feel kind of far away, like I can see them but can’t quite get to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, teaching kind of requires you to make about a hundred tiny decisions before lunch, so it’s hard to know what’s “me” and what’s just the job.\n\nLike yesterday I spent way too long deciding whether to move a reading group to the carpet or keep them at their desks, but that might just be because the classroom was noisy and someone had spilled glue near the cubbies, and then the whole room smelled like—well, anyway.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed a clear change in my thinking or anything. Maybe I feel mentally tired, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not concentrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or increased fatigue, even after resting or getting enough sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually kind of why I came in. I’m tired all the time, and not just in the normal “teacher at the end of the day” way, because, I mean, everyone at school is tired—especially after indoor recess, which is its own little disaster.\n\nBut this feels different. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up and still feel like I have to drag myself through everything. Grading papers, making dinner, packing my bag, getting ready for the next morning… it all feels like I’m pushing through mud or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating more or less than usual or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, not in any clear way. My eating is kind of all over the place just because the school day is weird—sometimes lunch is like seven minutes standing by the copier, and sometimes I forget I packed yogurt and then find it warm later, which is… not great.\n\nBut weight-wise, I haven’t noticed anything big. My clothes seem to fit the same, I think. I haven’t been trying to change anything. So… no, I don’t think there’s been a real appetite or weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down or hopeless that you've thought about hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I feel really low and kind of wonder how I’m supposed to keep doing everything—like when the copier jams five minutes before dismissal and then a parent email comes in and it’s somehow about snack policy from three weeks ago—but that’s more like feeling overwhelmed, I guess.\n\nI haven’t been thinking about hurting myself. And I don’t think I’ve felt like life isn’t worth living, not in that specific way. It’s more… I don’t know, just hard sometimes.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant tiredness and the gray, down feeling—almost every day?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and a couple months.\n\nIt started around when my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole routine at home changed. Not that it’s his fault exactly—sorry, that sounds like I’m blaming him, and I’m not trying to. It’s just, everything shifted then. Like mornings, chores, who’s where, who needs what… and somehow I feel like I’ve been carrying this gray tired feeling since around then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things like your hobbies, time with loved ones, or relaxing after work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still do the main things. I go to work, I’m there for my students, I come home and do what I’m supposed to do with my family. So it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning.\n\nBut I’ve definitely pulled back from things that used to make me feel more like… me. I used to mentor newer teachers more, stay after and help them plan or talk through classroom stuff, and now I kind of avoid taking that on if I can. Not because I don’t care, I just don’t have much left.\n\nAnd socially, I say no more. Like if people from work are getting coffee or someone invites us over, I’ll think, “That sounds nice,” and then by the time it gets close I’m already looking for a reason not to go. Even little creative routines I used to like—setting up my planner in a fun way, making classroom materials, doing little projects at home—I just don’t really keep up with them anymore.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But yes, I think I still show up for the important stuff, just… a lot of the fuller, enjoyable parts have gotten smaller.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything is kind of in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I feel like I drag sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “moving slowly” like you mean. At school I’m still walking around the room, getting kids lined up, answering questions, all of that. Though sometimes I’ll be standing at the board and realize I’ve been holding the marker cap for way too long—actually, that happens more when the students are all talking at once and I’m trying to remember who needed to go to the nurse and who lost their Chromebook login…\n\nSorry. I guess the short answer is I’m not sure I’ve noticed everything being in slow motion. It’s more just… getting through the day feels like a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving much more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless, even when you're not particularly anxious or excited?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really noticed.\n\nI mean, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve kind of always done that—like clicking a pen or straightening papers on my desk. And teaching, you’re constantly moving anyway. Half the day I’m walking around the room or crouching next to someone’s desk, or trying to remember where I set down the attendance folder… which I swear disappears on its own.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I feel like I can’t sit still, and I don’t think people have said I’m moving unusually slowly either. It’s more that I feel worn out, if that makes sense, but not like my body is in slow motion or racing around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt down or really low, not just for a day or two, but for weeks at a time—maybe with some of the same feelings you're describing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s not just recent. It’s been kind of hanging around for, um… a little over two years now, I think. About two years and maybe two months.\n\nIt sort of started after my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. Not that it’s all his fault or anything—sorry, I don’t mean it like that. It was just… suddenly the rhythm of the house was different, and I was adjusting to work and home feeling different at the same time. And somewhere in there, I realized I’d been feeling low for weeks, then months, and then it just kind of became the background.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression before this current period—like feeling down, losing interest in things, or having low energy—that lasted for several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everyone does, I guess—end of the school year can make you feel like you’re running on fumes, and there was one year with a really difficult class where I cried in my car a couple times, which sounds dramatic, but teachers do that more than people think.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it was depression for several weeks or anything clear like that. I’m having trouble separating normal stressful times from… whatever this is. So I guess I can’t really point to a definite episode before this one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss or a significant decrease in your appetite over the past two years, even if you weren't trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had weight loss, no. At least not anything I’ve noticed or that my clothes would be telling me, you know? My jeans are still… unfortunately honest.\n\nAppetite-wise, it’s kind of hard to say because school days are weird. Sometimes I don’t eat much lunch just because there are twelve things happening at once, and by the time I sit down someone needs help with a Chromebook or there’s a parent email I forgot to answer. But that’s been teaching forever, not really a new thing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a significant decrease in appetite or lost weight without trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or think clearly during the past two years, even when you're not particularly stressed or tired?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, as a teacher I feel like my brain is always juggling seventeen things at once—attendance, lesson plans, someone needing a bandaid, someone crying because their pencil broke, and then an email comes in about a meeting that could’ve been an email… or was already an email, actually.\n\nSo I do have moments where I feel scattered, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s just the job. When I’m not stressed or tired… I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear problem with focus, no. At least not something I could point to and say, “That’s new.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in your life before this current two-year period when you felt down or low for a week or more, and it was clearly different from your usual mood, not just a reaction to something stressful happening at the time?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know. I’ve definitely had times where I was in a funk for a few days, maybe even a week-ish, but it’s hard to separate that from, like, normal life stuff.\n\nLike there was a stretch in college around finals where I basically lived on coffee and vending machine pretzels, but that feels pretty obviously connected to finals, not some mysterious mood shift. And teaching has its own little seasons where everyone is exhausted and the copier jams daily like it has a personal grudge.\n\nSo… before this current stretch, I can’t really point to a clear time where I felt low for a week or more and it was definitely different from my usual mood for no obvious reason. I’m not sure I have an example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in your life when you felt down or depressed for several weeks or more, and then after that, you felt back to normal for at least a couple of months before this current period started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really coming up with a clean example of that. Like, several weeks depressed and then clearly normal for a couple months… I don’t know.\n\nI can think of times I was worn down, especially during different school years, but then it’s hard because the school calendar kind of chops life into weird pieces. You get winter break and you think, “Oh, I’m fine,” but also you’re just sleeping in and not grading fractions quizzes, so is that normal or just… vacation?\n\nSo I guess my answer is I’m not sure, but nothing obvious comes to mind where I’d say yes, that definitely happened before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "additional_requirements": [
      "No clear previous episodes of depression with full remission before this current period",
      "Absence of manic or hypomanic episodes",
      "Absence of psychotic features"
    ]
  }
}
